# The lab's ledger

Every lab job run on Google Cloud since 10 November 2026, whether it led anywhere or not, so nothing is run twice by mistake.
Jobs live in the bucket `gs://dandan-lab-807207481983` (project `project-b753c78c-ef88-46f1-b37`): `jobs/<id>.json` is the
spec (with `purpose` and, once read, `outcome`), `results/<id>/` what each shard wrote. `node lab/ledger.mjs` on a lab machine
lists them all with their progress. Earlier runs (GitHub Actions, up to 9 November) are in README.md's Matches section.

The workers fill the queue themselves when it runs dry (`LAB_FILL=1`): the next self-play batch `spb<n>` (120 games, Hard 256
deals against itself, seeds 7000 + 120(n−1) on) and, for each finished batch, `jt<n>`, the judge trained on batches 1..n.

| `lab/swings.mjs`: the judge against Korbi's kept games (game-records branch) | 39 replayable games (Monte won 27), judge at each turn start, both hands known | not calibrated badly in the middle (judge 35–50%: human won 39%; 50–65%: 52%) but overconfident for the human's leads (80–90%: won 65%); 10–20%: human won 1 of 24. A **sawtooth**: the verdict moves 10 points a half-turn and each move is half undone by the next (correlation −0.55); at the start of one's own turn the judge gives that player 8.1 ± 0.3 points less than on the turns either side. A judge that understood the game would only move on news |

Likely learned from Medium's games, where holding these goes with losing. Fix: a training term that a card in hand never lowers its holder's score |

### 11 Nov, 19:40Z: stage 1 model trained; G0 passed; G1 and G2 queued
- **aa-a1-pt1** trained on the export: 332,991 training and 85,734 held-out decisions, 121 inputs, 6 epochs, 364 s, 1.5 GB. Best at epoch 6.
- **G0 passed.** On the held-out Deep decisions, value given up against the search's best move fell from **1.47 points a decision (Medium) to 0.76 (the model): 48% less**. The bar was 25%.
- The model picks the search's top move less often than Medium does (70.4% against 76.3%). Where it differs, it picks moves the search valued nearly as well, so it gives up less value on average.
- α fell from 3.33 to 3.03 and β from 10 to 0.01: the model no longer leans on Medium's own pick, only on Medium's scores and the learned correction.
- Caveats:
  - the search's means at 1,024 deals are noisy;
  - the held-out games come from the same two lines of self-play;
  - at run time the model weighs only passing, Medium's pick and Medium's top five, while the export listed every legal move.
  G1 and G2 test it in play.
- **Queued on cb02856:**
  - **aa-a1-g1**: the model against Medium, 20,000 pairs, 100 shards; passes at 55%.
  - **aa-a1-g2**: Hard at Quick with the model in its look-aheads against Hard at Quick, both v14, 1,000 pairs, 200 shards; passes when the 99% range is wholly above 50%.
- Speed is not yet logged per side. It will be measured separately before a Deep gate (plan: under 25% slower).

## 11 November 2026, ~19:55Z: G1 failed badly; the model player fixed and retested

- **G1 (aa-a1-g1), model vs Medium, no search: 16.4% ± 0.5 (99%), 20,000 pairs.** Failed (bar 55%).
- Cause, found locally on 20 games: the model played *pass* where Medium played a land 433 times and where Medium cast 161 times.
  In the Deep data a move held to the next priority is worth about the same as playing it now (the search plays it later),
  so the model learned holding ≈ acting; as a player that compounds into never acting. G0 could not see this: it scores one
  decision with the search's own continuation.
- Fix (09444c7, dandan-netpolicy.js makePlus): lands stay Medium's; when Medium acts the model picks which move, not whether
  to pass. Local check: 40/80 games against Medium (even; noisy).
- G2 (aa-a1-g2) ran on the unfixed player: about 49% over 430 pairs (hand-tallied, rough). Retired; requeued as aa-a1-g2b on 09444c7.
- Queued: aa-a1-g1b (G1 again, 20,000 pairs), aa-a1-g2b, and for Korbi aa-a1-vd-plus (model alone vs Hard Deep v14, 100 pairs)
  with its yardstick aa-a1-vd-med (Medium alone vs the same, same deals).
- hard-match.mjs gained "plus@<job>" and "medium" sides (6c6f6d4).

  2.6 at 1.6× the time (the range just touches 50%). Halving the look-ahead to buy deals is out.
- Hard does notice who plays its play-outs: the 84–16 loser costs about 3 points (range wholly under 50%), far less than its
  34-point gap alone. The earlier "about 49% on 430 pairs" was this, seen through noise.

## 12 Nov — exact endgame calculator (Fable's option 3), with every possibility
- `dandan-solve.js`: double-dummy search to the end of the game (alpha-beta, a table of positions), a vote over deals, shuffles averaged over 3 orders, Memory resolving cut to the value net, loops a draw. `choices()` offers every possibility: every stop of both turns and every step (new `fullStops` in dandan-engine.js, off outside the calculator, so the game is unchanged), every response on the stack, every land set that pays, holding priority, a land tapped for mana when the stack aims at it, every Brainstorm put-back in every order, every discard/attack/block/order, a search that finds nothing, Predict hit or miss, Sanctuary's card or none.
- `tests/solve.mjs` (2.9 s): brute force over 103 late decisions, every kind of question: 38,436 legal moves and answers, **0 missing**. Left out only where a choice that is in does as much (over-tapping; floating mana nothing aims at; not holding priority = holding, then passing).
- **Size: it does not finish.** Kept game 1793961568018, 3–4.5 s per position, about 10,000 positions a second: to the end of the game, capped even with the library cut to 1 card. With a horizon at the end of the current turn: cut positions (hands 5+3 or 3+2, library 1–3) solve in 0.06–2 s, but the game's own late positions (library 12, turn 46) don't finish even that. Root choices up to 74 (855 at one test decision). keyOf is 24% of the time.
- `lab/endgame-solve.mjs` is the measurement (Lab workflow ready); not run on the fleet, as the local probe already shows the answer.

After Dandan's bar: (6) the pattern as a loop that needs only cards and mechanics, (7) matchup analysis, (8) card evaluation, including cards not in the deck, (9) every matchup and every theme, (10) a deck builder that climbs from a few given cards.
- **Rules (Fable, not struck by Korbi):** learned over hand-written: a learned piece replaces a hand-written one when its score is above 50% and the bottom of its 99% range above 49%; everything else needs the bottom above 50%. Live goes in piles: a pile goes to the Deep gate against the live version at about 4 points of Quick gains, or when Korbi asks; a pile under 4 points gets a third look at 2,400 pairs. The cycle as a standing order: every 15,000 new benchmark games (~480 core-hours) retrain the benchmark's recipe and the current variants, screen each in three looks, crown by Korbi's ruling, switch self-play to the new benchmark, one line to Korbi; nothing live. Report card: Monte's share of the last 100 real games with its 99% range, split by live version.

(2) **Old Hong Kong VMs win the refill race:** they poll every 30 s on the old worker and queue 24-shard S4 d batches with the built-in spec, while current workers back off to 120 s when idle, so nearly every new batch since 16:10Z (sps024–040) is a 24-shard S4 d batch from the old code. Hong Kong VMs cut 50 → 1 at 16:22Z; the last one keeps doing it until the pool is deleted (Korbi's call).
- 16:30Z **Idle again 16:10–16:28Z (director):** (1) exec's filler change set shards 240 and `games` 1,200 but left the args' literal `128 120`, so 120 games over 240 shards: half the shards played none. Director fixed the args to `128 1200` (3f016af). The args should take `{games}` (not done: a running worker substitutes only {seed}). (2) Backed-off pollers (120 s) let the old Hong Kong VMs (built-in filler, 30 s polls, 24 shards, S4 d) make nearly every batch, sps024–040. Fix: with LAB_FILL the poller keeps POLL_MS; the 120 s backoff applies only to machines without the filler. A refill guard in new code can't stop old workers; deleting the Hong Kong VM pool does. Seeds: old batches step 120 and new ones 1,200 from the same base and the same batch counter, so a new batch m (seeds from 1,200m) can only meet an old batch n ≥ 10m: none once the old VMs are gone, and those are S4 d games anyway (different engine).
- 16:40Z **fleet/open.json (director's requirements):** idle pollers read one file (class B, ~$0.0004/1,000) naming the jobs that may have a free shard, and look only at those; a full look at the queue when the file is missing, unreadable, older than 5 min, or has said "nothing" for 5 min (rule 1). Full looks rewrite the list (and touch it); targeted looks take out finished/full jobs and put in ones with free or lapsed shards; refill adds its batch, and if the last batch still has free shards the list missed, adds that; queue-push adds new specs; fleet.yml (tools/fleet-publish.mjs) adds every job the snapshot finds with a free or lapsed shard, only adding. All writes are generation-matched with retries (rule 4). Batch creation stays on the 15 s cadence, run only when the list was empty (rule 2). Workers report bucket requests since their last report (`req`: list, write, read, ms); the summary gives per-group and total requests per hour and $/h (class A $0.005/1,000, class B $0.0004/1,000) (rule 3). lab/open-list.mjs; lib.mjs store gained getG and conditional put. One restart (rule 5): Kubernetes pods rolled at push; VMs drain within 10 min plus their shards in hand.

- 16:45Z **Two faults, both exec's:** (1) refill compared the last batch's claims with the spec's new 240 shards, so after old 24-shard batches it never saw the last batch as taken and made none (director queued sps042 by hand at 16:40Z); now it uses the last batch's own shard count. (2) The forced pod restarts (16:14Z, 16:31Z, 16:34Z) killed workers whose claims stayed for 15 min (LEASE_MS): at 16:38Z 89 of 646 slots busy with nearly every shard of sps008–041 held by gone pods. Now a stopping worker (SIGTERM: spot reclaim, pod restart) deletes its claims and puts their jobs on the open list. No more forced restarts: this push rolls out by drain (each machine finishes its shards, then restarts on main within ~10 min plus its longest shard).
- 16:55Z **Request burst (director: $2.50/h of bucket requests at 16:44Z, Hong Kong 16-core $1.39/h, Sydney VMs $0.63/h):** not the claim path. A local test (16 slots, a 240-shard job) costs 2 lists, 4 writes and ~1.2 reads a shard, ~$0.08/h fleet-wide at today's shard rate. The cost was full looks: jobs/ holds 1,331 finished jobs, and a full look listed each one's claims and done marks (~2,700 lists) because `finished` is per process; every slot of a freshly started machine made one at once, and the 2-minute report window turned that burst into a big hourly rate. Fixed: fleet.yml writes fleet/finished.json (the snapshot's finished jobs) and workers skip those (re-read every 10 min); a starting machine looks with slot 0 only, the rest waiting at the gate. Workers log their total bucket requests when they stop.

- 23:03Z **Fable No. 9 points 4–5 built.** (5) The NumPy move-picker trainer: lab/fetch-ex.mjs packs an export's every-move decisions from the bucket into flat binary files, one part at a time. lab/policy_np.py trains form mlp (mpN's: dandan-netpolicy.js's layout, with no Medium-score weights) or form head. Same recipe as policy-train: pick target, Adam 1e-3, batches of 64, weight 1, 6 epochs, the best held-out epoch kept. Its report adds No. 8 point 1: agreement by the kind of the search's move beside Medium's, and passing where the search acted / acting where it passed, for the model and for Medium. Workflow train-mp-np.yml runs it on GitHub. (4) The policy head: a trunk reads the position once (XN → 128 → 64, ReLU), each of the 80 marks has a learned vector of 64 and a bias, and a move scores trunk · (sum of its marks' vectors) + their biases. dandan-netpolicy.js gains freeScorer(model) for both forms; makePlus plays a medium:false model through it. On synthetic decisions JavaScript and NumPy give the same scores (both forms, to 4 decimals), and both forms learn the planted rule (94% / 93% held out). mpN plays as before: a plus-match pair and a hard-match pair ran, and the filler's self-play game ended code 0 before the push. lab/mp-time.mjs now times the whole net through freeScorer, so the per-move rate figure in my 22:53Z entry was inflated. mpN's true cost is about 210 µs a decision (view 63, list 15, marks 10, net 123) against Medium's 83 µs, about 2.5×. Dispatched on GitHub: **mpNnp** (form mlp, 128·64, mpN's data: Fable's check, held-out agreement within 0.3 points of 81.3%) and **mpH** (form head, 128·64, the same data).

- 16:15Z **Scry 2d** (Korbi 16:00: the hands they could still hold; struck means replaced): lab/zero/scry2.mjs SlotFilter. Each hand names the other player's unknown cards slot by slot (each physical card followed from when it came in); a card seen leaving keeps the hands naming it there (a hand naming it on another slot swaps the two), strikes the rest; draws are dealt into each hand from what it leaves unseen; telling decisions (the hands disagree at least 2 to 1) are logged with the slots in hand then; struck and spent hands are replaced by copies of standing ones, each moved one card by a Metropolis step judged on the log. Ceiling test (4 games of an opponent playing the policy's top move, true card's loss a card): pool's odds 2.815, Scry 1 2.705, 2d at the policy's own softness and ε 0.03 about the pool (each decision says little: the true hand explains the move made 0.715 on average against 0.628 for a hand of the set), **2d with the policy sharpened (temperature 0.1) and a floor of 0.5: 2.613** (7.2% better than the pool, 3.4% better than Scry 1); grid: τ 0.1 ε 0.3 2.650, τ 0.25 ε 0.5 2.701, τ 1 ε 0.5 2.843, ε 0.7 2.803. Defaults now n 300, τ 0.1, ε 0.5 (a pair at Quick about 70 s). Queued az-s2d (vs blind, 300 pairs). g6x-C training (zero-train-ext, games az-g5-c,az-g6-c). Old nets still byte for byte.

## 17 Nov 00:10Z — XMage read, first pass (plain clone of magefree/mage, depth 1, no footprint)
1. Dealing hidden cards again in a copy: yes, already done. Its own MCTS player (Mage.Player.AIMCTS) copies the game
   (createSimulationForAI = full copy) and for every other player shuffles hand into library and draws the same number again
   (MCTSNode.randomizePlayers). Naive: ignores cards known from reveals, scry or tutors; our belief would replace it. A world on
   another decklist needs card objects made from the card database, not just moved: more work, needed only for the deck layer.
2. One choice at a time: half. Every choice goes through the Player interface (priority, choose, chooseTarget, chooseUse,
   chooseMode, announceX, getAmount, attackers, blockers, trigger and replacement order, mulligan), so all are reachable. But they
   are callbacks made from inside resolving rules code: the engine waits on the call, and the Java call stack is not part of the
   copyable state. XMage's MCTS branches only at priority, attackers and blockers, and answers every other question at random in
   play-outs. To search a question we would copy at the last priority point and re-run with a script of forced answers per branch.
   This is exactly the defect we just measured in our own engine (questions answered by a quick head, not searched).
3. Speed: a copy deep-copies the whole game state and every card object in the game (gameCards, LKI maps, effects, triggers):
   for 120 cards with abilities, likely milliseconds, against microseconds in our engine. To be timed before any decision on it.
